Text:"SUBMITTED TO IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON PATTERN ANALYSIS AND MACHINE INTELLIGENCE 1 Motion Mamba 2.0: Autoregressive Diffusion Mamba for Long-Horizon Humanoid Control Yue Huang, Tianshan Zhang, Zeyu Zhang, Jayeon Kang, Akide Liu, Sungjoon Choi, Dacheng Tao, Fellow, IEEE, Hao Tang AbstractLong-horizon, real-time controllable motion generation is essential for interactive animation and humanoid robotics, where an agent must respond to changing instructions and spatial goals while moving. Existing autoregressive diffusion methods generate motion incrementally, but bounded attention caches limit access to distant history, while repeated denoising introduces substantial inference overhead. We present Motion Mamba 2.0, Mamba-based rootbody diffusion architecture that integrates local attention, resumable recurrent states, and persistent content memory (PCM) for long-horizon controllable motion generation. PCM transforms compressed motion history from Mamba recurrent states, together with state changes and text-conditioned features, into dynamically updated content slots accessible to local attention. To enable efficient inference, we further develop an execution scheme tailored to this architecture, combining CUDA Graph replay, fused kernels, and execution-flow simplification while preserving the canonical history update. Evaluations on retargeted HiPHI demonstrate accurate terminal control over rollouts of up to 39.52 s, while short-horizon comparisons on BONES Seed and retargeted HumanML3D show that the model also retains strong spatial control and textmotion matching. On an RTX 4090, the optimized execution reduces median complete-window latency from 1685.35 to 276.14 ms, achieving median paired speedup of 6.138 while preserving identical outputs and committed states in the tested rollouts. Moreover, we conduct extensive real-world evaluation on the Unitree G1, demonstrating sequential text-guided action composition, expressive motion, and interactive locomotion through keyb"

Text:"Benchmarking Physical Agency Xinyang Zhou, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang xML Lab, National University of Singapore AI systems can now operate Blender, follow instructions in complex video games, and manipulate objects with robot arms. What range of physical goals can todays AI systems achieve autonomously? Answering this question requires testing systems on tasks that demand different combinations of perception, memory, planning, and action. We introduce WorldLeague, benchmark that compares AI systems on common set of 60 simulated robot tasks by measuring progress toward each tasks physical goal from the robots trajectory. Robots explore scenes to gather evidence in WorldSeek, rearrange objects in WorldShape, and adapt to changes in WorldShift. Tying these goals to single control interface limits which AI systems can be compared. In WorldLeague, vision-language models (VLMs) choose targets and motions, while vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs) use their native controls. For each of six capability dimensions, we measure how much progress system makes toward its goals (Interactive Score) and how often it achieves them (physical success rate). Together, these measures form the systems Physical Agency Profile. Among 13 representative AI systems, Claude Fable 5.2 achieves 13 of the 60 goals and earns the highest mean Interactive Score (28.58/100). Fable leads in four dimensions, while GPT-6.1-Sol and GPT-6-Astra tie for first on rule-following tasks. Even when VLMs answer every final question correctly, they receive an Interactive Score of zero in 71.8% of those episodes. WorldLeague gives Embodied AI, Physical AI, and Agentic AI shared basis for comparing what AI systems achieve autonomously, from partial progress toward physical goal to full goal completion. Existing harnesses such as VISTA improve the first two, yet turn each model call into at most one action, so the agent acts only as often as its model thinks. We introduce AutoPILOT, which changes the third: in each model call, the model can send primitive action, start an open-loop macro, or write closed-loop controller that keeps playing from the screen without further model calls until it hands control back. Latent action models (LAMs) can recover these dynamics, but they train their inverseand forward-dynamics models (IDM, FDM) from scratch, trading off pixel reconstruction against latent capacity and accuracy. Pretrained video world models natively generate dynamics and produce high-fidelity subsequent frames, yet represent them densely, entangled across the hidden states of every layer. Viewing the world model as LAM, we show that these dynamics can be extracted with minimal changes to the model. Native LAM operates one pretrained video diffusion transformer as both IDM and FDM, with learnable dynamics registers as the only causal bridge between the two, and trains on counterfactual temporal pairs that differ in motion alone, so that the registers distill videos dynamics into compact native latent action. We further propose pretraining paradigm for vision-language-action (VLA) policies, Pretraining Actions from World models (PAWlicy), in which Native LAM serves as dynamics teacher on large-scale video; unlike world action models, it generates video neither during training nor during inference. Native latent actions outperform existing LAMs on the LARY benchmark in both action classification and control regression, and replay the same motion on new robots, in new scenes, and from human hands to robots, although no transfer objective is used in training. With 3.8B model, PAWlicy reaches 93.6% average success rate on RoboTwin 2.0 trained with domain-randomized demonstrations, the highest among VLAs. On RoboDojo, it achieves"

Text:"What Makes Good Data for Training Coding Agents? Systematic Study on SWE-Gym Yikai Zhang 1 Mai Xu 2 Yunbei Zhang 3 Qirui Jin 4 Kenan Li 5 Yuwen Jia 6 Jinwei Yao 1 Xiangyu Zhang 1 Jielun Tan 1 Jin Pan 1 Zijian Jin 7 Xiao Sun 1 Code ı Dataset Blog1. Introduction Reinforcement learning (RL) trains coding agents on software tasks whose hidden tests decide the reward, yet task collections check these tests only against the reference fix. We ask what makes task good training data, focusing on the correctness of its reward labels. We audit SWE-Gym with two LLM families, and suspected error counts only if execution reproduces it. We find that unsolved often means mislabelled: 74% of the tasks that 9B base model never solves have test that rejects correct fixes (a false negative), versus 20% of those it sometimes solves. We repair or remove such tasks, and the audited tasks that remain train better policies. At equal size and matched difficulty, models trained on them score 2.4 and 2.7 points higher on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro than models trained on the original tasks, whereas simply dropping the never-solved tasks shows no such gain. We further find that wrong patches that pass (false positives) make up about quarter of all passing patches, and that new tests that catch quarter of them do not change held-out scores detectably. pass rate shows whether task yields reward, not whether that reward is right, so the reward labels themselves must be checked. To this end, we release SWE-Gym-Verified with its 1,087 audited tasks, every verdict with its executed evidence, our training sets, and the full audit pipeline. 1AMD 2University of Michigan 3Tulane University 4Georgia Institute of Technology 5University College London 6University of California, Los Angeles 7New York University. Correspondence to: Yikai Zhang <yikzhang@amd.com>. Preprint. November 23, 2026. 1 Reinforcement learning (RL) has become standard way to train coding agents (Luo et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2026), and task collections such as SWE-Gym, R2E-Gym, SWE-smith, and SWE-rebench supply the training tasks (Pan et al., 2025; Jain et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2025). In collections mined from GitHub, task pairs repository and an issue with the tests of the pull request that resolved it (Jimenez et al., 2024). The agent sees the issue but not the tests, and its patch is rewarded only if the tests pass. The hidden tests are therefore the reward labels. Yet collection admits task after checking only that the reference fix passes the tests and the original code fails them. This check shows that the reward is attainable. It does not show that every correct fix obtains it, or that only correct fixes do. Tests can mislabel patch in two ways (Fig. 1a, b). false negative (FN) is correct fix that the tests reject, for example because test calls private helper that the issue never mentions. false positive (FP) is wrong patch that the tests accept, for example one that handles the reported case but breaks nearby code path that no test covers. Both are well documented in evaluation benchmarks, where even human screening misses many of them (Chowdhury et al., 2024; OpenAI, 2026b; Wang et al., 2026c). Training data receives coarser checks. RL recipes drop the tasks that the model never solves (Yang et al., 2026) or the groups of samples without reward variance (Yu et al., 2025b), without looking at the tests. pass rate tells whether task yields reward, not whether that reward is right. LLM judges label each tasks issue and tests only as whole (Oliva et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2025; 2026). What is missing is an execution-backed measurement of both error types in real training set and controlled test of their effect on RL. We ask what makes task good training data for coding agent, and answer in two steps  (Fig. 2)  . First, we audit SWE-Gym (Pan et al., 2025) one fail-to-pass assertion at time. Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol judge each assertion against the issue. flagged assertion counts as an error only if the tests reject an executed alternative fix that follows the issue, and passing patch counts as false positive only if What Makes Good Data for Training Coding Agents? Systematic Study on SWE-Gym Figure 1. Hidden tests mislabel patches in two ways, and the two error types occur in different places. (a) false negative in iterative/dvc-3490: the test relies on private method and private flag that the issue never names. (b) false positive in pandasdev/pandas-50796: base-model patch passes every original test but ignores into=, which counterexample exposes. (c) Share of Orig tasks that are FN-flawed and share of passing patches on Clean tasks that are false positives, by the number of eight base-model samples that pass. For false positives, counts passes among the eight samples drawn for that measurement. an executed counterexample exposes it. Tasks with false negative are repaired by adding the missing requirement to the issue, or removed. The 1,087 remaining tasks form SWE-Gym-Verified. Second, we train 9B base model with GRPO on three sets of 512 tasks with matched difficulty: original tasks (Orig), audited tasks (Clean), and audited tasks with added tests against false positives (Clean+FP). fourth set, Orig+Filter, applies pass-rate filter to the original tasks, dropping the never-solved ones. We evaluate every run on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro (Deng et al., 2026). We organize the study around four research questions: RQ1: Where do label errors occur? Unsolved often means mislabelled (5.2). In Orig, 74% of the neversolved tasks have test that rejects correct fixes, versus 20% of the sometimes-solved ones. False positives make up about quarter of all passing patches and are common at every difficulty level (Fig. 1c). RQ2: Which label errors reach the training signal? Pass-rate filters keep the label errors that train the policy (5.3). GRPO learns from task only if its samples rewards differ. Never-solved flawed tasks almost never carry gradient, so dropping them changes little. Sometimes-solved flawed tasks contribute fifth of the gradient-carrying groups, with or without the filter. RQ3: Do audited tasks train better policies? Yes, and pass-rate filter does not substitute for the audit (5.4). At equal size and matched difficulty, Clean scores 2.4 points above Orig on SWE-bench Verified and 2.7 on SWE-bench Pro over three seeds. Averaged over both "

Text:"A Pipeline-Parallel System for Depth-Recurrent Models Yunjia Xi1, Jiarui Qin1, Di Yin1, Xing Sun1 1Tencent Youtu Lab Depth-recurrent (Looped) models raise effective depth by applying shared stack of blocks several times. Scaling them usually requires pipeline parallelism, but commonly used interleaved 1F1B is drawn for an acyclic stage graph in which each micro-batch enters each stage once. Once that stack is split across stages, the same stage is visited several times on one micro-batch and later stages feed earlier ones: native 1F1B has no slot for the extra visit, cannot rank first iteration against second, and its neighbour-only send/receive order has no back edge to the first stage, so the arrangement stalls or fires out of order. We present Loop-PP, which rebuilds both the timetable and the communication for this cyclic layout. It schedules different iterations of the same stage separately, specializes 1F1B skeleton into globally consistent order over those visits, and emits static instruction stream per rank that separates posting from waiting so communication overlaps local compute. Loop-PP is bit-identical to single-stage reference. On 8B-60B MoE models, it lowers peak memory by as much as 32.8%, and with compute budget fixed, the best observed throughput rises by 12.7%. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have been driven primarily by scaling model parameters (Kaplan et al., 2020; Brown et al., 2020), data (Hoffmann et al., 2022), and test-time computation (Snell et al., 2024). Looped Transformers or depth-recurrent Transformers offer different path by recursively applying the same blocks, increasing effective model depth and capacity without adding parameters (Dehghani et al., 2019; Lan et al., 2020; Geiping et al., 2025; Zhu et al., 2025). Recent studies have shown that such models hold promise for reasoning, in-context learning, and test-time scaling (Saunshi et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2024; Alabdulmohsin and Zhai, 2025). Existing work, howe"

We present Wactorz, an open-source runtime in which LLMgenerated agents are long-lived, supervised actors: planner turns chat request into Python, compiles it into new actor at runtime, and supervisor restarts that actor alone, with its state intact, when it crashes. All coordination rides on MQTT, so agents can be spawned on, and live-migrated between, edge nodes such as Raspberry Pi, and every internal event is topic external systems can subscribe to. The recorded demo builds camera-to-lamp automation from one chat request, adds second agent that is auto-wired to the first by topic and carries deliberate division-by-zero, watches that agent repair its own code without losing its count, then deploys fresh Raspberry Pi node and live-migrates the agent onto it, in under four minutes of wall-clock time and $0.13 of model usage. This extended version adds what the two-page demonstration paper could not: the Wiring view of the topic graph, Wactorz as library for hand-written agents, how trained models are stored, sent to nodes and replaced, the security model, and the systems limitations. Text:"ReWeave: Emergent Physical Task Topologies for Embodied Harness Optimization and Cross-Task Meta-Distillation Yicheng Xu1, Wencan Jiang1, Zihao Zhu2,4, Keru Zhou3, Chang Zhou4, Li Liu4, Maoqing Yao4, Yong Liu1, Jiangning Zhang1, 1 Zhejiang University, 2 Stanford University, 3 Tsinghua University, 4 AGIBOT Innovation Corresponding author Long-horizon embodied tasks involve physical dependencies that may only become clear through interaction. As task understanding deepens, past trials can reveal new information for adaptation and transfer. However, coarse progress feedback cannot identify which physical preconditions held, leaving search unguided, while task-specific optimization easily overfits to individual scenes, making cross-task transfer brittle. We introduce ReWeave, framework that jointly optimizes an embodied agent harness and an emergent physical task topology from interaction without updating policy weights. Keeping execution policies and perception frozen, ReWeave co-optimizes coupled context, workflows, and control loops. As new evidence revises the topology, ReWeave reprojects past trials onto the updated task understanding, exposing retained progress, regressions, and bottlenecks that guide parent selection and search width. Across tasks, matching verified local mechanisms to unmet preconditions consolidates 72 specialist units into 44 reusable groups (0.34 full concatenation) within compact shared harness capable of continual learning. Text:"Feedback Shortcut: Speculative Decoding for Latent Feedback Transformers Benhao Huang (cid:97), Xuezhe Ma, Hector Liu Institute of Foundation Models, USC, CMU (cid:97) Code: https://github.com/ifm-ai/LoRM Date: November 20, 2026 (cid:97) Correspondence: huskydogewoof@gmail.com Overview Feeding each positions top-layer hidden state back into the next positions input improves Transformer performance. However, this feedback breaks speculative decoding, since verifying drafted token requires the top-layer state from its preceding position, which forces the draft to rely on an approximate stand-in. Training the model to adapt to this stand-in can lower the exactdecoding performance ceiling, while using it at inference time further introduces approximation error. We propose Feedback Shortcut to address both bottlenecks. First, State alignment trains lightweight projection from multi-token prediction head states to stand-ins, leaving base model training untouched. Second, Pipelined correction re-evaluates each draft using its predecessors newly computed statea much closer proxy to true feedback. Running this correction inside the verification pass of subsequent drafts improves accuracy with zero extra calls. Across 135M and 4 bits per byte of the exact-decoding ceiling on 780M scales, Feedback Shortcut stays within 10 Paloma and accelerates decoding by 2.42.8 for only 2% extra training compute. At large batch sizes, skipping correction still preserves Transformer speed and over 80% of latent feedbacks gains. Text:"NoHairCache: Calibrate-Once KV Cache Eviction with One Power-Law ExponentAbstract The decay exponent α in KV-cache eviction is an intrinsic property of the model: calibrated once on single corpus, it transfers directly across domains. Attention-based accumulation (H2O) and recency-based sliding windows (StreamingLLM) are two dominant eviction frameworks, often treated as unrelated, but we show that they are the two endpoints of single power-law family: by scoring each token as si = Ai (t i)α, where Ai is cumulative attention and α controls the age penalty, H2O and StreamingLLM correspond to α = 0 and α , respectively (with StreamingLLM additionally hard-coding an attention-sink patch). The no-hair mechanism of Xiang [2026] bounds the admissible range of α, and the measured exponents agree: calibrating once on WikiText yields [0.556, 0.822] for representative model (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct). The per-model ranges are consistent across five models (410M1.5B) and independently match the optimal regions from perplexity sweeps. NoHairCache achieves the lowest perplexity across three datasets (WikiText, PG19, and arXiv) without per-dataset tuning, outperforming both H2O and StreamingLLM (p < 0.05 on all three datasets, with 0.060.10 PPL gains over StreamingLLM at budget 512). On downstream four-way multiple-choice QA, it matches or outperforms StreamingLLM across all nine (task, budget) cells, retaining 98.2% of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at 25% retention versus 91.1% for StreamingLLM; per-cell McNemar tests are not significant, so QA is corroborating only. We release our calibration script and drop-in scorer upon publication.Long-context inference is often bottlenecked at generation time: every decoded token has to attend to the entire KV cache, causing memory usage to grow linearly and, as result, total attention compute to scale quadratically with sequence length. Caching every past key-value pair is therefore untenable, making token eviction unavoidable. The question now is: by what criterion should tokens be evicted? Existing eviction policies rely on diverse scoring heuristics: accumulated attention (H2O) [Zhang et al., 2023], recency windows (StreamingLLM) [Xiao et al., 2024], observation-window pooling (SnapKV) [Li et al., 2024b], query-aware relevance (Quest) [Ge et al., 2024], or even random selection. Two paradigms dominate in practice: accumulation-based methods (e.g., H2O) and recency-based methods (e.g., StreamingLLM) [Zhang et al., 2023, Xiao et al., 2024]. Although power-law decay of token influence has been observed empirically [Lin and Tegmark, 2017, Xiang, 2026], these two paradigms remain disconnected heuristics without unified parameterization. In this work, we show that they are in fact opposite endpoints of single power-law family. By scoring each cached token at decoding step as si = Ai (t i)α, (1) where Ai denotes cumulative attention and α 0 governs the age penalty, we are able to unify both strategies under single framework. The H2O and StreamingLLM scoring rules then correspond to 1 the endpoints α = 0 and α , respectively (StreamingLLM additionally hard-codes an attention sink). Consequently, designing an eviction policy reduces from choosing between competing heuristics to identifying the decay exponent α of this family, which we calibrate per layer. The exponent is an intrinsic property of the model architecture rather than the input data distribution. Measuring α for each model once on single calibration corpus (e.g., WikiText-103) yields consistent means and bulk ranges across models (such as Qwen2.5-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B), although the detailed layer-wise profiles are only partly preserved; the measured values align closely with optimal perplexity sweeps, and each models frozen α transfers unchanged to distinct domains like PG19 and arXiv. To ground α theoretically, we draw on the empirical no-hair mechanism established in our prior work [Xiang, 2026], which shows that prompts hidden-state influence on position decays as tα, with α bounded layer-wise within [0.2, 0.7] by attention-dilution geometry. The theorem constrains the exponent within family but does not select the family itself. Building on this insight, we introduce NoHairCache, lightweight, drop-in scorer that replaces the scoring function of top-k eviction policies with our power-law formulation based on offlinecalibrated, per-layer α values, incurring only single scalar multiplication per eviction step over H2O. In summary, we make the main following contributions: Intrinsic, Calibrate-Once Decay Profiles. We demonstrate that the per-layer decay exponent α is an intrinsic property of the model rather than the corpus: across five models its mean lies in 0.6020.663, and single offline calibration transfers without re-tuning across WikiText, PG19, arXiv, cache budgets, and downstream QA. We further show why hiddenstate influence avoids the softmax recency bias that underestimates α in attention-mass proxies. Unified Power-Law Framework. We relate accumulation-based (H2O) and recency-based (StreamingLLM) KV-cache eviction as opposite endpoints (α = 0 and α ) of single power-law decay family si = Ai (t i)α; the family is modeling choice, not consequence of the no-hair theorem, which only constrains the exponent within family. Lightweight Drop-In Algorithm (NoHairCache). We propose training-free, plug-andplay eviction scorer that integrates per-layer calibrated exponents into standard top-k eviction pipelines with negligible overhead (a single scalar multiplication per step over H2O). Cross-Domain and Downstream Gains. Across three corpora (WikiText, PG19, arXiv) and three long-context QA benchmarks (NarrativeQA, HotpotQA, Qasper), NoHairCache improves perplexity with statistical significance (paired Wilcoxon < 0.05 on every dataset), while on QA it is never worse than StreamingLLM (e.g., retaining 98.2% of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at 25% cache budget vs. 91.1% for StreamingLLM) though the gains are not statistically significant (per-cell McNemar). Echoing the no-hair principle in black hole physics, which argues that complex objects collapse down to just few fundamental parameters, our findings suggest that the seemingly complex behavior of KV cache eviction policies is largely governed by single intrinsic power-law form, whose exponent profile is fixed once per model. Rather than designing increasingly intricate heuristics, future cache compression methods can build directly on "

Abstract  Long-horizon, real-time controllable motion generation is essential for interactive animation and humanoid robotics, where an agent must respond to changing instructions and spatial goals while moving. Existing autoregressive diffusion methods generate motion incrementally, but bounded attention caches limit access to distant history, while repeated denoising introduces substantial inference overhead. We present Motion Mamba 2.0, a Mamba-based root-body diffusion architecture that integrates local attention, resumable recurrent states, and persistent content memory (PCM) for long-horizon controllable motion generation. PCM transforms compressed motion history from Mamba recurrent states, together with state changes and text-conditioned features, into dynamically updated content slots accessible to local attention. To enable efficient inference, we further develop an execution scheme tailored to this architecture, combining CUDA Graph replay, fused kernels, and execution-flow simplification while preserving the canonical history update. Evaluations on retargeted HiPHI demonstrate accurate terminal control over rollouts of up to 39.52 s, while short-horizon comparisons on BONES Seed and retargeted HumanML3D show that the model also retains strong spatial control and text-motion matching. On an RTX 4090, the optimized execution reduces median complete-window latency from 1685.35 to 276.14 ms, achieving a median paired speedup of 6.138times while preserving identical outputs and committed states in the tested rollouts. Moreover, we conduct extensive real-world evaluation on the Unitree G1, demonstrating sequential text-guided action composition, expressive motion, and interactive locomotion through keyboard and mouse control.
[24.11.2026 13:33] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлена Motion Mamba 2.0 — архитектура root-body диффузии на основе Mamba для генерации длинных, управляемых в реальном времени движений. Модель объединяет локальное внимание, возобновляемые рекуррентные состояния и persistent content memory (PCM), которая превращает сжатую историю движений в динамически обновляемые слоты памяти. Для ускорения инференса применяются CUDA Graph replay, fused kernels и упрощение потока выполнения, что снижает задержку почти в 6 раз без изменения результатов. Эксперименты показывают точное управление на длинных горизонтах до 39.5 с, сохранение пространственного контроля и соответствия тексту, а также работу на роботе Unitree G1.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Долгая память движения: управляемая генерация движений в реальном времени"}

"Abstract  Long-horizon, real-time controllable motion generation is essential for interactive animation and humanoid robotics, where an agent must respond to changing instructions and spatial goals while moving. Existing autoregressive diffusion methods generate motion incrementally, but bounded attention caches limit access to distant history, while repeated denoising introduces substantial inference overhead. We present Motion Mamba 2.0, a Mamba-based root-body diffusion architecture that integrates local attention, resumable recurrent states, and persistent content memory (PCM) for long-horizon controllable motion generation. PCM transforms compressed motion history from Mamba recurrent states, together with state changes and text-conditioned features, into dynamically updated content slots accessible to local attention. To enable efficient inference, we further develop an execution scheme tailored to this architecture, combining CUDA Graph replay, fused kernels, and execution-flow simplification while preserving the canonical history update. Evaluations on retargeted HiPHI demonstrate accurate terminal control over rollouts of up to 39.52 s, while short-horizon comparisons on BONES Seed and retargeted HumanML3D show that the model also retains strong spatial control and text-motion matching. On an RTX 4090, the optimized execution reduces median complete-window latency from 1685.35 to 276.14 ms, achieving a median paired speedup of 6.138times while preserving identical outputs and committed states in the tested rollouts. Moreover, we conduct extensive real-world evaluation on the Unitree G1, demonstrating sequential text-guided action composition, expressive motion, and interactive locomotion through keyboard and mouse control."

Abstract A systematic framework shows that frontier multimodal models can serve as robot policies when harnessed with interfaces favoring native visual reasoning and tool use, achieving high success in simulated and real manipulation tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Frontier multimodal models exhibit extraordinary generalization in generating robot actions from observations and instructions, enabling a new paradigm in which they serve directly as policies within closed-loop robotic harnesses. However, the design principles of such harnesses, and the capabilities underlying their strong performance, remain unclear. We present Embo-Harness, a framework for systematically studying frontier-model-as-policy harnesses. We investigate four components of the robotic agentic loop, namely, prompting, planning, action execution, and tool use, and distill six empirical findings into a practical recipe. This recipe prioritizes interfaces that exploit the model's native visual reasoning and tool-use capabilities over handcrafted plans, skills, or action priors, and identifies effective choices for action representation, chunking, reasoning effort, visual observations, short multi-action sequences, and policy refinement. On RoboLab-120, Embo-Harness achieves an 83.3\% success rate, outperforming the strongest reported baseline by 26.2 percentage points. It also succeeds in 15 of 20 zero-shot trials across four real-world manipulation tasks. Beyond performance, controlled interventions indicate that the studied model depends more strongly on accurate spatial target estimation than on explicit low-level joint or trajectory generation. These results provide both a practical recipe for building strong frontier-model-as-policy harnesses and a clearer account of the capabilities that drive their performance.

Авторы систематически исследуют четыре компонента цикла: prompting, planning, action execution и tool use, и выводят практический рецепт, где приоритет отдаётся нативному визуальному рассуждению и использованию инструментов, а не handcrafted-планам и skills. На бенчмарке RoboLab-120 метод достигает 83.3% успешности, превосходя лучший известный baseline на 26.2 п.п., а в реальных задачах манипуляции показывает 15 успешных zero-shot запусков из 20. "Abstract A systematic framework shows that frontier multimodal models can serve as robot policies when harnessed with interfaces favoring native visual reasoning and tool use, achieving high success in simulated and real manipulation tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Frontier multimodal models exhibit extraordinary generalization in generating robot actions from observations and instructions, enabling a new paradigm in which they serve directly as policies within closed-loop robotic harnesses. However, the design principles of such harnesses, and the capabilities underlying their strong performance, remain unclear. We present Embo-Harness, a framework for systematically studying frontier-model-as-policy harnesses. We investigate four components of the robotic agentic loop, namely, prompting, planning, action execution, and tool use, and distill six empirical findings into a practical recipe. This recipe prioritizes interfaces that exploit the model's native visual reasoning and tool-use capabilities over handcrafted plans, skills, or action priors, and identifies effective choices for action representation, chunking, reasoning effort, visual observations, short multi-action sequences, and policy refinement. On RoboLab-120, Embo-Harness achieves an 83.3\% success rate, outperforming the strongest reported baseline by 26.2 percentage points. It also succeeds in 15 of 20 zero-shot trials across four real-world manipulation tasks. Beyond performance, controlled interventions indicate that the studied model depends more strongly on accurate spatial target estimation than on explicit low-level joint or trajectory generation. These results provide both a practical recipe for building strong frontier-model-as-policy harnesses and a clearer account of the capabilities that drive their performance."

Abstract A new benchmark evaluates rigging and motion generation across six 3D morphology categories, compares specialized, video-based, and agent-driven paradigms, and proposes an agentic framework that writes compact parametric skeleton and motion programs executed deterministically for reliable control.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Animating arbitrary 3D assets from instructions requires both structural inference and motion control, yet existing motion benchmarks mostly cover conventional human or animal skeletons with simple instructions. We introduce DivDynBench, which evaluates rigging and motion generation across six morphology categories, with instructions requiring multi-phase part coordination and explicit timing, angle, repetition, and trajectory constraints. With large language models, we systematically compare three common paradigms: specialized motion generation, video generation followed by motion recovery, and direct agent-driven generation. We analyze their control capabilities, cross-morphology adaptability, and generation costs. Guided by this comparison, we propose an Agentic Motion Framework in which an agent writes compact parametric skeleton and motion programs, and a deterministic executor performs rigging, kinematic solving, and geometric correction. Compact parameter control reduces generation overhead and improves rigging reliability and motion-control accuracy, providing practical guidance for deploying complex 3D motion generation.
[24.11.2026 13:33] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют DivDynBench — новый бенчмарк для оценки риггинга и генерации движения 3D-объектов шести морфологических категорий, где инструкции требуют координации нескольких фаз и явного контроля времени, углов, повторов и траекторий. Сравниваются три парадигмы на базе LLM: специализированная генерация движения, генерация через видео с последующим восстановлением движения и прямая агентная генерация. По результатам сравнения предложен Agentic Motion Framework, в котором агент пишет компактные параметрические программы скелета и движения, а детерминированный executor выполняет риггинг, решение кинематики и геометрическую коррекцию. Abstract WeMM-Ecom, a family of multimodal large language models spanning dense and mixture-of-experts sizes, is trained with continued pre-training and hybrid-thinking post-training to achieve strong e-commerce and general-purpose results and is deployed in WeChat.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash We present WeMM-Ecom, a family of multimodal large language models that deliver frontier e-commerce capabilities while achieving state-of-the-art general-purpose performance at their respective scales. The family spans five sizes, from a 0.8B dense model to a 35B mixture-of-experts model with 3B active parameters. Our training pipeline begins with two-stage continued pre-training that progressively raises the proportion of e-commerce data within a diverse multimodal data mixture, followed by hybrid-thinking post-training that combines on-policy distillation and reinforcement learning to support both non-thinking and thinking modes. For evaluation, we construct a comprehensive suite of 28 e-commerce benchmarks (23 in-house and five open-source) covering eight business scenarios and complement it with 64 public general-purpose benchmarks. On the e-commerce suite, WeMM-Ecom-35B-A3B surpasses substantially larger open-source flagship models, including Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, HY4, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. On the general-purpose suite, every WeMM-Ecom model surpasses its similarly sized Qwen3.5 counterpart, with the lightweight variants (e.g., WeMM-Ecom-2B) showing clear advantages over leading lightweight models such as Qwen3.5-2B, MiniCPM-V-4.7-1B, and LFM3-VL-2B. Beyond benchmarks, WeMM-Ecom now powers hundreds of production use cases in WeChat, including catalog management, multimodal product search, and livestream commerce, serving billion-scale requests per day. We publicly release all model weights and detail our data curation, training recipe, and benchmark construction to facilitate future work on domain-specialized multimodal models. We have released the model weights and code to facilitate future research at https://github.com/Tencent/WeMM-Ecom.

'emoji': emoji that will reflect the theme of an article somehow, only one emoji. 'title': a slogan of a main idea of the article in Russian. Return only JSON and nothing else.

Abstract A code localizer is trained with scores from a frozen fixer via weighted supervised fine-tuning, improving bug localization and patch success for a small coding agent without tests or reinforcement learning.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Code localizers are usually trained to predict the files a reference patch edits, but the code a fixer needs to read is not the same as the code it changes. We propose Fixer-Guided Localization (FGL): the localizer proposes several localizations, a frozen fixer scores each by how likely it makes a verified patch, and the localizer is trained on these scores with weighted supervised fine-tuning, without running tests and without reinforcement learning. We build the full pipeline on Gemma 4 E4B for the Kaggle Gemma-4 Developer Agent competition, from 1,629 test-verified Kimi K3 trajectories. The FGL target helps the fixer find the bug as often as the teacher's localization while using 27.5% less code. On the development set, one EM round moves the agent in the expected direction compared with a matched control: more tasks reach a patch (53 vs. 46), fewer tasks get stuck reading files that do not exist (43 vs. 54), and more tasks are solved (8 vs. 6). Training also changes how the agent works: trained agents explore about three times more than the base model and write far fewer wrong patches, and the single agent solves up to 11 of 78 tasks, against 8 for the base model. An analysis of 13 evaluation runs identifies what limits a 4B agent under an 8-minute budget (time, repetition loops and edit quality) and gives practical lessons for training small coding agents. All data, code and adapters are publicly available.

Локализатор генерирует несколько вариантов локализации, а fixer оценивает, насколько каждый из них повышает вероятность успешного патча. Обучение проводится через weighted supervised fine-tuning без запуска тестов и без reinforcement learning, на модели Gemma 4 E4B. Эксперименты показывают, что FGL помогает находить ошибки не хуже учителя, используя на 27.5% меньше кода, и улучшает работу небольшого coding agent.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Учим локализатор читать код глазами того, кто его исправляет"}

Abstract WorldLeague benchmarks 13 AI systems on 60 simulated robot tasks spanning exploration, rearrangement, and adaptation, measuring progress and success to compare autonomous physical goal achievement.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash AI systems can now operate Blender, follow instructions in complex video games, and manipulate objects with robot arms. What range of physical goals can today's AI systems achieve autonomously? Answering this question requires testing systems on tasks that demand different combinations of perception, memory, planning, and action. We introduce WorldLeague, a benchmark that compares AI systems on a common set of 60 simulated robot tasks by measuring progress toward each task's physical goal from the robot's trajectory. Robots explore scenes to gather evidence in WorldSeek, rearrange objects in WorldShape, and adapt to changes in WorldShift. Tying these goals to a single control interface limits which AI systems can be compared. In WorldLeague, vision-language models (VLMs) choose targets and motions, while vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs) use their native controls. For each of six capability dimensions, we measure how much progress a system makes toward its goals (Interactive Score) and how often it achieves them (physical success rate). Together, these measures form the system's Physical Agency Profile. Among 13 representative AI systems, Claude Fable 5.2 achieves 13 of the 60 goals and earns the highest mean Interactive Score (28.58/100). Fable leads in four dimensions, while GPT-6.1-Sol and GPT-6-Astra tie for first on rule-following tasks. Even when VLMs answer every final question correctly, they receive an Interactive Score of zero in 71.8\% of those episodes. WorldLeague gives Embodied AI, Physical AI, and Agentic AI a shared basis for comparing what AI systems achieve autonomously, from partial progress toward a physical goal to full goal completion. Project Webpage: https://xyangzhou.github.io/WorldLeague
[24.11.2026 13:33] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет WorldLeague — бенчмарк для сравнения 13 AI-систем на 60 симулированных робототехнических задачах, включающих исследование сцены, перестановку объектов и адаптацию к изменениям. Для каждой из шести измерений возможностей оценивается прогресс к цели (Interactive Score) и доля успешно выполненных задач (physical success rate), которые вместе формируют Physical Agency Profile системы. VLM выбирают цели и движения, а VLA и WAM используют собственные интерфейсы управления. Результаты показывают, что даже при правильных ответах VLM часто получают нулевой балл, потому что не переходят к физическим действиям.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Сколько физических целей AI-система достигает сама?"}

Abstract nanoAstra combines semantic action modeling with capability-preserving supervised fine-tuning to train vision-language models for generalizable robot manipulation, achieving strong zero-shot success and faster streaming execution.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Recently, GPT-6 Astra demonstrates strong generalization in robot control, yet limited disclosure of its training leaves open how such capabilities can be reproduced. We introduce nanoAstra, an approach to training vision-language models (VLMs) for generalizable manipulation that combines semantic action modeling with capability-preserving supervised fine-tuning (CP-SFT). nanoAstra represents robot behavior as semantic steps composed of reusable, parameterized action primitives, guided by explicit progress descriptions. Meanwhile, CP-SFT uses an explicit robot-specific system prompt to constrain manipulation outputs to a structured action space, aiming to focus adaptation on robot-specific contexts and promote specialization while minimizing interference with broader pretrained capabilities. With this formulation, we train nanoAstra on more than 5,000 hours of robot data together with general VQA data. On RoboTwin 2.0's randomized benchmark, nanoAstra-9B achieves a 17.8% zero-shot success rate, substantially outperforming advanced VLA and WAM baselines as well as similarly sized VLMs. With inference faster than the corresponding motion execution on a single H20 GPU, our model supports action-chunk-like streaming execution that overlaps inference with motion, reducing the robot's idle time from 35.0% to 6.9%.

Abstract ViDecide trains video diffusion models to make causal state-transition and stopping decisions, using Corrective Fine-tuning to improve multi-step planning success without policy gradients.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Current leading video generation models can synthesize highly realistic videos, while still frequently violating physical and logical constraints in multi-step reasoning and planning, even after training with large-scale reasoning data or existing diffusion RL methods. We attribute these failures to the conventional video training paradigm, which treats a solution as a fixed-length clip rather than a chain of decisions. We present ViDecide, a decision-centric framework that trains video diffusion models to predict state transitions causally and decide when to stop, with a decision-focused training schedule. We further propose Corrective Fine-tuning (CFT), which corrects failed decisions by mapping their noise to the model's own successful continuations, achieving stable improvement without policy-gradient optimization. Across abstract games, planning tasks, and a new benchmark of multi-step robot planning, ViDecide achieves an average strict success rate of 83.8%, far above 23.3% for standard fine-tuning and 67.8% for the strongest prior method, and generalizes to larger puzzles and unseen layouts. Our framework therefore unlocks greater potential for video generation models as reliable reasoners and planners in the visual world. Project page: https://kevin-thu.github.io/ViDecide.

Вместо обучения на фиксированных по длине клипах модель рассматривает решение как цепочку решений, что улучшает многошаговое планирование. Abstract A harness that lets multimodal foundation models emit primitive actions, open-loop macros, or closed-loop controllers improves real-time game performance and reduces API cost.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal foundation models can play video games well when the game waits for them, but often fail when it keeps running. We find that much of this gap lies in the harness, which decides what the model sees, what it remembers, and how its outputs become actions over time. Existing harnesses such as VISTA improve the first two, yet turn each model call into at most one action, so the agent acts only as often as its model thinks. We introduce AutoPILOT, which changes the third: in each model call, the model can send a primitive action, start an open-loop macro, or write a closed-loop controller that keeps playing from the screen without further model calls until it hands control back. To evaluate agents in real-time games, we build AFK-Bench, 120 diverse browser games grouped by how they respond to inaction and playable in real time or with the clock paused. With GPT-6.1 Sol under the same budgets, AutoPILOT reaches 68.6% real-time success against 38.3% for VISTA, and on GameWorld it keeps 80.0% in real time while VISTA drops from 84.1% to 42.9%. Its gains hold across models, and it reaches VISTA's final success rate at a fifth of its API cost. Our results suggest that how a harness turns model outputs into actions over time is a central yet underexplored part of agent design, and we hope AutoPILOT and AFK-Bench will serve as a foundation for agents in a world that does not wait.

"Abstract A harness that lets multimodal foundation models emit primitive actions, open-loop macros, or closed-loop controllers improves real-time game performance and reduces API cost.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal foundation models can play video games well when the game waits for them, but often fail when it keeps running. We find that much of this gap lies in the harness, which decides what the model sees, what it remembers, and how its outputs become actions over time. Our results suggest that how a harness turns model outputs into actions over time is a central yet underexplored part of agent design, and we hope AutoPILOT and AFK-Bench will serve as a foundation for agents in a world that does not wait."

Abstract Native LAM distills compact latent actions from a pretrained video diffusion transformer via learnable dynamics registers, and PAWlicy uses these actions to pretrain VLA policies without generating video, achieving strong robotic control and cross-embodiment transfer.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Large and diverse video sources, such as egocentric human video and heterogeneous robot recordings, carry rich dynamics but come either without actions or without a shared action space. Latent action models (LAMs) can recover these dynamics, but they train their inverse- and forward-dynamics models (IDM, FDM) from scratch, trading off pixel reconstruction against latent capacity and accuracy. Pretrained video world models natively generate high-fidelity dynamics, yet represent them densely, entangled across all layers. Viewing the world model as a LAM, we show that these dynamics can be extracted with minimal changes to the model. Native LAM operates one pretrained video diffusion transformer as both IDM and FDM, with learnable dynamics registers as the only causal bridge between the two, and trains on counterfactual temporal pairs that differ in motion alone, so that the registers distill a video's dynamics into a compact native latent action. We further propose a pretraining paradigm for vision-language-action (VLA) policies, Pretraining Actions from World models (PAWlicy), in which Native LAM serves as a dynamics teacher on large-scale video; unlike world action models, it never generates video. Native latent actions outperform existing LAMs on the LARY benchmark in both action classification and control regression, and replay the same motion on new robots, in new scenes, and from human hands to robots, although no transfer objective is used in training. With a 3.8B model, PAWlicy reaches a 93.6% average success rate on RoboTwin 2.0 trained with domain-randomized demonstrations, the highest among VLAs. On RoboDojo, it is competitive on every dimension we evaluate, and its Precision, evaluated by us, is on par with the leading leaderboard entries. On real robots, it improves over the pi_0.5 baseline on both tasks. Both stages use only public data and a publicly released world model.

Модель обучается на контрфакторных парах кадров, различающихся только движением, и извлекает из видео компактные латентные действия. На их основе предложен метод предобучения VLA-политик PAWlicy, где видео-модель выступает учителем динамики без генерации видео. Abstract  Learning robot manipulation from egocentric human videos requires accurate metric 3D hand motion. Recent methods leverage pretrained video diffusion model (VDM) priors to recover occluded hands directly from full-frame videos, but their appearance-based depth regression generalizes poorly to unseen cameras. In this work, we propose ViDiHand2, a geometry-driven framework that reformulates VDM-based hand depth estimation from direct regression into fine-grained geometric constraint solving. Specifically, we introduce Fine-Grained Geometric Lifting and Clip-Level Ray Fitting, which recover metric depth by enforcing geometric consistency between pixel-aligned 2D joints and the metric structure of MANO hands, while leveraging temporal observations across the clip. Since the lifting relies on these 2D joints, to improve robustness under occlusion and image truncation, we further develop Confidence-Gated Fusion to adaptively combine geometrically lifted joints with MANO predictions according to localization confidence. Finally, we enhance the VDM backbone to better capture spatial-temporal representations and produce the per-frame 2D joint and MANO predictions that both steps rely on. Extensive experiments on seven public datasets demonstrate that ViDiHand2 consistently outperforms 18 baselines, reducing in-the-wild absolute joint error by 42% and detection failures by 8.82x compared with the best baseline on each metric, while running at 57 FPS. The code is available at https://github.com/NTUYWANG103/ViDiHand.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract  Learning robot manipulation from egocentric human videos requires accurate metric 3D hand motion. Recent methods leverage pretrained video diffusion model (VDM) priors to recover occluded hands directly from full-frame videos, but their appearance-based depth regression generalizes poorly to unseen cameras. In this work, we propose ViDiHand2, a geometry-driven framework that reformulates VDM-based hand depth estimation from direct regression into fine-grained geometric constraint solving. Specifically, we introduce Fine-Grained Geometric Lifting and Clip-Level Ray Fitting, which recover metric depth by enforcing geometric consistency between pixel-aligned 2D joints and the metric structure of MANO hands, while leveraging temporal observations across the clip. Since the lifting relies on these 2D joints, to improve robustness under occlusion and image truncation, we further develop Confidence-Gated Fusion to adaptively combine geometrically lifted joints with MANO predictions according to localization confidence. Finally, we enhance the VDM backbone to better capture spatial-temporal representations and produce the per-frame 2D joint and MANO predictions that both steps rely on. Extensive experiments on seven public datasets demonstrate that ViDiHand2 consistently outperforms 18 baselines, reducing in-the-wild absolute joint error by 42% and detection failures by 8.82x compared with the best baseline on each metric, while running at 57 FPS. The code is available at https://github.com/NTUYWANG103/ViDiHand."

Abstract  Foundation models can reason about complex physical tasks, yet translating this intelligence into reliable robot execution remains challenging. We argue that a critical, often overlooked factor is the robot-use interface (RUI), which determines how agents express and execute physical actions. To systematically study its impact, we introduce RUIBench, comprising 30 manipulation tasks across five suites spanning world knowledge, in-context learning, discovery, dynamic interaction, and contact-rich manipulation. We further develop RUIKit, a unified framework that crosses six action abstractions with two interaction forms, yielding 13 interfaces under shared perception and control. In simulation, changing only the RUI shifts a frontier agent's success rate from 45% to 81%, and that of a fine-tuned 4B model from 13% to 47%. Our analysis reveals that effective interfaces reduce unnecessary decisions while preserving essential feedback, with their benefits shaped by agent behavior and physical uncertainty. We further uncover a verification gap, where agents frequently declare success despite observable failures, revealing the need for task-level verification beyond individual actions. Together, these findings establish RUI design as a critical factor in embodied agent reliability.

Abstract A few selected interaction trajectories combined with on-policy distillation allow a small visual agent to transfer broadly and recover most of a larger teacher's performance with minimal online experience.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash How much interaction experience is needed to distill a capable visual agent? We study learning from few tasks and recorded interaction histories. Our framework selects representative trajectories using skill-context coverage and ordered visual-decision similarity. On-policy distillation (OPD) learns through fresh interaction on selected tasks or fresh student responses at fixed histories. Across Navigation, Sokoban, and spatial mapping, even one online training task supports transfer to other tasks. Four fixed trajectories raise a 4B student's success from 49.17% to 82.08% on standard Navigation and from 60.16% to 85.55% on Sokoban, recovering over 90% of the measured gap to a 27B teacher on these panels. On an independent Navigation panel, replay improves success from 45.83% to 76.67%, retaining 91.36% of the same-task online gain without new interaction during optimization. Training repetitions, matched supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and a 9B extension further support the value of compact experience.

"Abstract A few selected interaction trajectories combined with on-policy distillation allow a small visual agent to transfer broadly and recover most of a larger teacher's performance with minimal online experience.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash How much interaction experience is needed to distill a capable visual agent? We study learning from few tasks and recorded interaction histories. Our framework selects representative trajectories using skill-context coverage and ordered visual-decision similarity. On-policy distillation (OPD) learns through fresh interaction on selected tasks or fresh student responses at fixed histories. Across Navigation, Sokoban, and spatial mapping, even one online training task supports transfer to other tasks. Four fixed trajectories raise a 4B student's success from 49.17% to 82.08% on standard Navigation and from 60.16% to 85.55% on Sokoban, recovering over 90% of the measured gap to a 27B teacher on these panels. On an independent Navigation panel, replay improves success from 45.83% to 76.67%, retaining 91.36% of the same-task online gain without new interaction during optimization. Training repetitions, matched supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and a 9B extension further support the value of compact experience."

Abstract A discrimination-aware latent-space learning framework combines task guidance with on-policy retrieval feedback to preserve discriminative evidence in multimodal embeddings and improve retrieval across modalities and tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal large language models have greatly improved input understanding in universal multimodal embedding models. However, this understanding does not ensure that their embeddings preserve the evidence needed to distinguish relevant items from confusable alternatives. To preserve essential discriminative evidence in embeddings, this paper proposes DiLE, a discrimination-aware latent-space learning framework that combines task guidance with on-policy retrieval feedback. Specifically, a few learnable queries are used to encode task-relevant information in a compact latent space. Reasoning instructions and auxiliary answer-generation supervision shape these representations to support both task understanding and retrieval discrimination. To address task-specific discriminative needs, we design shared-private expert adaptation that integrates cross-task knowledge sharing with task-specific representation learning. Building on this, the on-policy hard-negative distillation is introduced: the student retrieves confusable candidates, and a reverse KL divergence objective aligns its embedding-based similarity distribution with the teacher's fine-grained relevance distribution over these candidates. Candidates are periodically refreshed using updated student snapshots, tying teacher supervision to the student's evolving retrieval behavior and confusions. Across four model configurations that span two backbone generations, DiLE achieves state-of-the-art performance on MMEB-v2, UVRB, and MRMR, demonstrating its effectiveness across modalities and tasks.

Обучаемые запросы кодируют задачно-релевантную информацию в компактном представлении, а shared-private expert adaptation объединяет общие знания между задачами с частными. Abstract  Vision Banana showed that a closed image generator, instruction-tuned to paint perception outputs as RGB images, becomes a generalist vision model. Does this carry over to open generators, and if not, what fails? We answer with Open Vision Banana (OVB), an open framework that adapts the 7B diffusion generator of Qwen-Image-2.1 into one instruction-driven model for metric depth, semantic, instance, and referring segmentation, using a single shared low-rank adapter and public data. Each task is a task contract whose RGB encoder, instruction, and decoder share one specification and are checked before training. Studying 42 externally evaluated checkpoints from two 100k-update runs, six exact weight-space merges, and eight controlled shorter runs, we find that the gap to the closed system is specific and measurable. Geometry is learned early while absolute scale is not: median-aligned NYUv2 δ_1 stays at 0.89--0.93 throughout training as unaligned δ_1 wanders between 0.11 and 0.50. The scale is not committed by a single sample: a ten-seed ensemble lifts unaligned δ_1 from 0.348 to 0.516 without retraining. Depth and 2D segmentation compete for the shared adapter, and weight merging moves along this trade-off without escaping it. Finally, lower validation flow loss comes with lower Cityscapes accuracy (Spearman ρapprox-0.9 between loss reduction and mIoU), so the loss cannot select checkpoints. We release code, contracts, training data, and all evaluation outputs.

Abstract  Vision-language models have become a new kind of describer, writing about almost anything people see, and their descriptions now shape how cultures are known by people and by other models. Descriptions of cultures tend to turn particular things into types, and what a description loses shows what its describer lacks. We introduce Polytropos, a benchmark that measures what model descriptions of cultural objects keep of the particular object by turning each description back into an image and comparing it with the original photograph. On 374 objects from the Metropolitan Museum of Art and the Getty, with 1,870 descriptions from five models and 230 written by curators, reconstructions are recognised as the recorded culture more often than the photographs, at 54.55% to 59.09% against 52.14%, yet 16% to 21% of objects change outcome and only 0.49% of reconstructions are identified as the specific object. On the same objects, models lose recorded cultures at a rate similar to curators but gain more. Model descriptions give each object many faces, each typical of its culture and almost none the object itself.

Описание превращается обратно в изображение с помощью генеративной модели и сравнивается с оригинальной фотографией. Выяснилось, что реконструкции чаще распознаются как принадлежащие нужной культуре, но лишь в 0,49% случаев узнаются как конкретный объект. Abstract UUBench assesses how well multimodal interactive systems understand and respond to users' implicit communicative needs through process- and outcome-oriented scoring grounded in observable audiovisual evidence.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Effective real-time, full-duplex multimodal interaction requires systems to understand not only what users say, but also the communicative needs conveyed through prosody, facial expressions, gestures, and context, and to generate audiovisual responses that appropriately address those needs. Existing evaluations offer limited insight into whether systems translate an understanding of such unspoken needs into effective responses. We introduce UUBench, a benchmark for assessing how well multimodal interactive systems Understand the Unspoken intent and address users' implicit communicative needs. The inherent subjectivity of implicit intent inference poses a key challenge for evaluation. To address this, we introduce Process-Oriented Scoring, which grounds the assessment of intent fulfillment in observable auditory and visual cues, such as prosody and gestures. Rather than relying solely on a single overall score, it evaluates how well a response addresses the communicative needs conveyed by each applicable primitive dimension. We aggregate the dimension-level scores according to each dimension's relevance to the annotated evidence. Beyond intent fulfillment, Outcome-Oriented Scoring evaluates the naturalness, appropriateness, and human-likeness of the reply itself under audio-only, visual-only, and audiovisual conditions. To make these judgments traceable to observable evidence, we propose a Fact-Grounded Scoring JEV module, which uses structured, timestamped audiovisual observations of the user and the digital human's reply to assess one dimension or criterion at a time. UUBench provides a foundation for assessing whether multimodal interactive systems respond effectively to meaning beyond literal wording, supporting progress toward context-aware, real-time, full-duplex interaction.
[24.11.2026 13:35] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен UUBench — бенчмарк для оценки того, насколько мультимодальные интерактивные системы (например, LLM-based цифровые люди) понимают неявные коммуникативные намерения пользователя по интонации, мимике и жестам. Авторы вводят Process-Oriented Scoring, который оценивает выполнение намерения по наблюдаемым аудио- и визуальным признакам для каждой измерительной размерности с последующей агрегацией с учетом их релевантности. Дополнительно Outcome-Oriented Scoring оценивает естественность, уместность и человекоподобность ответа в аудио-, видео- и аудиовизуальных условиях. Модуль Fact-Grounded Scoring JEV опирается на структурированные временные метки наблюдений, чтобы сделать оценки прослеживаемыми и опирающимися на факты.", "emoji": "🎭", "title": "Понять невысказанное: оцениваем ИИ по тому, что человек не сказал вслух"}

Abstract An open-source agentic platform and benchmark enable iterative transformation of multimodal inspirations into playable game extensions, revealing that coding agents can adapt reference content but still struggle with playable interactions and precise modifications.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash AI-driven game development holds the potential to transform diverse human inspirations into interactive experiences. However, existing systems and benchmarks primarily focus on explicit development specifications, with limited support for adapting multimodal references and evaluating actual gameplay experiences. GameAnything integrates coding agents with an independent playtesting critic, persistent development state, and reusable experience memory to support continuous refinement. Abstract  RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes a continual process. Weight merging and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into a soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. A single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teacher's gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In a multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40x that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward continual post-training on a fixed backbone.

Авторы предлагают метод Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD): замороженная базовая модель, независимая дистилляция каждого эксперта в soft prompt и композиция промптов простой конкатенацией без совместного обучения. Один soft prompt восстанавливает не менее 90% прироста учителя в 22 из 23 задач по математике, коду и агентным сценариям, а композиция нескольких экспертов сопоставима с MOPD (67.83 против 67.79) и лучше обобщается на новые бенчмарки. Метод почти не увеличивает накладные расходы при инференсе, что указывает на возможность непрерывного post-training на фиксированной backbone-модели.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Собираем экспертов в модель из готовых промптов без переобучения"}

Abstract A shared flow-matching policy that reads dense patch tokens from multiple vision encoders improves success on egocentric bimanual tasks, and inverse-dynamics readout of commanded motion is a strong pre-training signal, while recognition, depth, and correspondence transfer unreliably.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Pretrained vision is a common start for robot learning, but most comparisons still score one pooled vector from a third-person image. Bimanual policies instead read dense patch tokens from cameras on the robot. We introduce HIVE-Bench: one shared flow-matching policy reads 20 encoders from seven families on 24 egocentric bimanual tasks, across adaptation, task sharing, and sweeps of data, model size, and layer. Keeping the patches matters. Averaging each view to one token removes most of the success, adaptation helps every pair we test, and one shared policy keeps most of the success of separate policies at a fraction of the updates. The same tokens are then scored with statistics, state and object readouts, published vision scores, and an inverse-dynamics readout. That readout of the commanded motion is one of the strongest measurements available before a policy is trained. Recognition, depth, and correspondence do not transfer reliably. Fine-tuning can improve the action readout even when other measurements move the other way, and no statistic is stable enough to replace a rollout before any deployment.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract A shared flow-matching policy that reads dense patch tokens from multiple vision encoders improves success on egocentric bimanual tasks, and inverse-dynamics readout of commanded motion is a strong pre-training signal, while recognition, depth, and correspondence transfer unreliably.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Pretrained vision is a common start for robot learning, but most comparisons still score one pooled vector from a third-person image. Bimanual policies instead read dense patch tokens from cameras on the robot. We introduce HIVE-Bench: one shared flow-matching policy reads 20 encoders from seven families on 24 egocentric bimanual tasks, across adaptation, task sharing, and sweeps of data, model size, and layer. Keeping the patches matters. Averaging each view to one token removes most of the success, adaptation helps every pair we test, and one shared policy keeps most of the success of separate policies at a fraction of the updates. The same tokens are then scored with statistics, state and object readouts, published vision scores, and an inverse-dynamics readout. That readout of the commanded motion is one of the strongest measurements available before a policy is trained. Recognition, depth, and correspondence do not transfer reliably. Fine-tuning can improve the action readout even when other measurements move the other way, and no statistic is stable enough to replace a rollout before any deployment."

Abstract Language models can edit their own neural network weights to improve task performance, though the resulting gains are poorly understood and sometimes matched by random search.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Frontier language models can now read and edit the weights of a neural network much like code and thereby improve a small model, an approach known as Weight-Space Programming (WSP). We further explore applying WSP to a language model itself, which we call Self-WSP. Specifically, Qwen3.5-0.8B reads its own weights, proposes an edit, and then continues with the edited weights as both problem solver and editor. We find many cases of genuine self-improvement. Self-edits can raise GSM8K accuracy from 11.8% to 62.2% and MATH500 accuracy from 7.0% to 32.2%, sometimes by changing only 16 parameters, and in some cases repeated self-editing keeps improving the model. A closer look, however, reveals that these gains lack interpretability. The model shows little understanding of the edits it makes, and WSP driven by random search sometimes performs comparably. Self-WSP thus holds promise as a path toward recursive self-improvement (RSI), but realizing this promise requires further exploration.

Abstract Auditing reward labels in coding-agent reinforcement learning tasks shows many unsolved tasks are mislabeled, and training on corrected tasks improves policy performance on SWE-bench benchmarks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Reinforcement learning (RL) trains coding agents on software tasks whose hidden tests decide the reward, yet task collections check these tests only against the reference fix. We ask what makes a task good training data, focusing on the correctness of its reward labels. We audit SWE-Gym with two LLM families, and a suspected error counts only if execution reproduces it. We find that unsolved often means mislabelled: 74% of the tasks that a 9B base model never solves have a test that rejects correct fixes (a false negative), versus 20% of those it sometimes solves. We repair or remove such tasks, and the audited tasks that remain train better policies. At equal size and matched difficulty, models trained on them score 2.4 and 2.7 points higher on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro than models trained on the original tasks, whereas simply dropping the never-solved tasks shows no such gain. We further find that wrong patches that pass (false positives) make up about a quarter of all passing patches, and that new tests that catch a quarter of them do not change held-out scores detectably. A pass rate shows whether a task yields reward, not whether that reward is right, so the reward labels themselves must be checked. To this end, we release SWE-Gym-Verified with its 1,087 audited tasks, every verdict with its executed evidence, our training sets, and the full audit pipeline.
[24.11.2026 13:36] Response: {"desc": "Авторы проверяют корректность наград (reward labels) в задачах для обучения coding-агентов через reinforcement learning на SWE-Gym. Они обнаружили, что задачи, которые 9B-модель никогда не решает, часто содержат ошибочные тесты, отвергающие правильные исправления (false negatives: 74% против 20% у задач, которые решаются иногда). Очищенный и отремонтированный набор данных при одинаковом размере и сложности даёт прирост на 2.4 и 2.7 пункта на SWE-bench Verified и SWE-bench Pro. Также выяснено, что неверные патчи, проходящие тесты (false positives), составляют около четверти всех прошедших патчей, а новые тесты не дают заметного улучшения метрик, поэтому авторы выпускают SWE-Gym-Verified с 1087 проверенными задачами и полным пайплайном аудита.", "emoji": "🔍", "title": "Низкий pass rate не значит, что награда верна: проверяйте метки!"}

We find that unsolved often means mislabelled: 74% of the tasks that a 9B base model never solves have a test that rejects correct fixes (a false negative), versus 20% of those it sometimes solves. We repair or remove such tasks, and the audited tasks that remain train better policies. At equal size and matched difficulty, models trained on them score 2.4 and 2.7 points higher on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro than models trained on the original tasks, whereas simply dropping the never-solved tasks shows no such gain. We further find that wrong patches that pass (false positives) make up about a quarter of all passing patches, and that new tests that catch a quarter of them do not change held-out scores detectably. A pass rate shows whether a task yields reward, not whether that reward is right, so the reward labels themselves must be checked. To this end, we release SWE-Gym-Verified with its 1,087 audited tasks, every verdict with its executed evidence, our training sets, and the full audit pipeline."

Abstract A dual-agent framework with hierarchical memory and recursive self-improvement enables robotic manipulation agents to convert execution evidence into reusable guidance without updating model parameters.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Vision-language model (VLM)-based agents offer a promising approach to robotic manipulation, yet remain limited by inadequate memory organization, uncertain failure attribution, and limited reuse of execution experience. We propose Embodied-Harness, a dual-agent framework that improves manipulation by refining the execution harness without updating model parameters. The Actor interacts with the environment through a robot-control interface, while the Reflector converts execution evidence into reusable operational guidance. Hierarchical Memory Architecture maintains short-term execution context and long-term experiential knowledge to support execution and reflection. Evidence-based Experience Reflection grounds failure diagnosis in observed interactions, while Recursive Harness Self-Improvement refines operational guidance across successive attempts. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0, RoboSPA, and RMBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Embodied-Harness in generalization under environmental variations, spatial reasoning, long-horizon planning, and memory-dependent manipulation. Our method achieves success rates of 82.4%, 76.7%, and 94.4% on the three benchmarks, respectively. Real-world evaluation further yields an average success rate of 96.7% across three manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of Embodied-Harness to improve robotic manipulation through experience-driven harness self-improvement.

Иерархическая память (short-term и long-term) хранит контекст и накопленный опыт, а Evidence-based Experience Reflection обосновывает диагностику ошибок наблюдаемыми взаимодействиями.
